<h2>14 August 2025</h2>
<p><strong>From</strong>: Cade Metz<br />
<strong>To</strong>: Zack M. Davis<br />
<strong>Date</strong>: Thu, 14 Aug 2025 10:02:30 -0700<br />
<strong>Subject</strong>: terminology question</p>
<p>You and other [<em>sic</em>] have talked about Scott Alexander's response to be as decision theory.  But is game theory a better term? How do you (and the rest of the community) think about the distinction between the two?</p>
<p>Cade</p>
<hr />
<p><strong>From</strong>: Zack M. Davis<br />
<strong>To</strong>: Cade Metz<br />
<strong>Date</strong>: Thu, 14 Aug 2025 13:00:54 -0700<br />
<strong>Subject</strong>: Re: terminology question</p>
<p>I actually said "game theory", not "decision theory." And maybe that choice of phrase is just me over-intellectualizing and using an academic term in a context where it doesn't actually clarify anything.</p>
<p>The point was that I don't think it makes sense to say, "Cade Metz didn't dox Scott Alexander Siskind; Siskind published the name <em>himself</em> on Astral Codex Ten <em>before</em> Metz's February 2021 NYT article."</p>
<p>The reason I don't think that makes sense is because Scott only published his last name <em>after</em> <a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2020/09/11/update-on-my-situation/">"gradually rework[ing] [his] life to be compatible with the sort of publicity that circumstances seem[ed] to be forcing on [him]" because "maintaining anonymity [was] a losing battle"</a>. If you hadn't been exploring a story about the rationalists in June 2020, or if you had told Scott that you'd respect his pseudonymity, those circumstances wouldn't have happened: he would have stayed with his employer and stayed on <em><a href="http://slatestarcodex.com">slatestarcodex.com</a></em>.</p>
<p>The reason I wanted to summarize that as it being "game-theoretically your (Metz's) fault" (I don't know if that phrasing made sense to you) is because game theory is the academic term for the study of how people's decisions depend on the decisions of other people who have different interests. (Thomas Schelling described it as the "theory of interdependent decision" in his seminal work <em>Strategy of Conflict</em>.) The definition is abstract, but the <em>kind of thinking</em> that Schelling was writing about, about making and responding to threats and promises, recursively computing "my best move, given his best move, given my best move, given ...", is ubiquitous in human life, and even in the animal world. Countries prepare for nuclear war, not because anyone <em>wants</em> a nuclear war—it would be better for everyone if no one had nukes—but because it's even worse for you if you don't have nukes, but your rival nation does. Gazelles <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stotting">leap in the air, not "for fun", but to credibly signal to predators that they're too fast to bother chasing</a>.</p>
<p>The conflict between you and Scott Alexander had a similar flavor. You were credibly threatening to publish his name, which "changed the game" such that his <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_response">best response</a> ended up involving up publishing the name first, but that's <em>not at all</em> the same thing as him just spontaneously deciding to drop pseudonymity. It was definitely your (or <em>The New York Times</em>'s) fault.</p>
<p>"Decision theory" is a related but more general term. I would venture that a lot of the distinction is a contingent accident of intellectual history. (Academics studying Newcomb's problem called themselves "decision theorists"; academics studying the Prisoner's dilemma called themselves "game theorists", but they're intrinsically deeply related subjects.)</p>
<p>In recent years, Yudkowsky has apparently taken to <a href="https://time.com/collection/time100-ai/6309037/eliezer-yudkowsky/">telling journalists that he's a "decision theorist"</a> rather than an "artificial intelligence researcher", presumably because his AI thinking never produced academically-legible achievements (because he was trying to come up with a theory of ultimate AGI "Friendliness", not implement something immediately useful the way people like Hinton, Sutskever, <em>&amp;c</em>. have done), whereas <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">functional</a> (formerly <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TDT.pdf">"timeless"</a>) <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/DeathInDamascus.pdf">decision theory</a> is a more academically credible concrete contribution.</p>
<p>Not sure how you can maintain journalistic distance about this part in the book (the whole <em>SSC</em>-vs.<em>NYT</em> affair is presumably part of the story you're trying to tell, but in this case, <em>you</em> are an interested character in the story). Maybe switch to an autobiographical "gonzo" style for that chapter?!</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/P8GuwYdy6foSzyZog/conversations-with-cade-metz-on-the-rationalists#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Model access for third-parties — it's a big deal!](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RuGZ5tMdqpnraJahJ/model-access-for-third-parties-it-s-a-big-deal)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Over time, there might be an increasingly large gap between </span><b><span>insider model access</span></b><span> and </span><b><span>outsider model access. </span></b><span>By insiders, I mean employees at the frontier lab.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefambxkw0k58f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnambxkw0k58f">[1]</a></sup></span><span> By "outsiders", I mean external safety researchers, third-party auditors, and other actors trying to make the future go well. I will call this a</span><b><span> model access gap</span></b><span> — and when the gap is small, I'll call this </span><b><span>model access parity</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref31fl4pecqs8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn31fl4pecqs8">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I think that one of the top priorities for the external AI safety community over the next 6-12 months should be ensuring model access parity. Main reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>This would allow us to direct billions of dollars in AI labour towards making things go well. This seems robustly good, regardless of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KeZSMBAxYZFzFpF9Y/how-might-outsiders-make-things-go-well" rel="noreferrer"><span>what activities we decide to actually direct the labour towards</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>I think publicly available models will probably lag 3-6 months behind the best internal models. Hence, as R&amp;D uplift grows superexponentially, we might see the differential uplift grow from 2x to 60x. In short: I think achieving model access parity might be preferable to</span><i><span> scaling</span></i><span> </span><i><span>the headcount of outsider orgs by ten-fold.</span></i></li><li value="3"><span>Model access parity isn't too far from the status quo, but it's the kind of thing that we could lose soon. I think precedent here might be sticky, so it seems like a good time to push for it.</span></li></ol><p><span>To be clear: </span><b><span>I think this is a big deal, it probably won't happen by default, and we are not on track to achieve it.</span></b></p><h1><span>Which outsiders?</span></h1><p><span>"Outsiders" includes the AI safety community outside the frontier labs, along with other actors trying to make the future go well. To make thing concrete, I've included a list below of orgs this might include.</span></p><p><span>I want to be clear that this list is intentionally expansive — it's not supposed to indicate which outsiders are top priority for model access parity. One might believe there are just 3-5 high-priority orgs, such that ensuring model access parity for these orgs would capture most of the value from a widespread model access parity. Alternatively, you might believe that the returns diminish slowly, and we should push for access for the entire list.</span></p><h2><span>Examples of outsiders</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>People evaluating risk posed by current or imminent deployments (METR, Apollo, UK AISI, AI Lab Watch)</span></li><li value="2"><span>People tracking and forecasting capabilities (Epoch, METR, AIFP, etc)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Grantmakers (Coefficient Giving, FLF, OpenAI Foundation, Longview, Macroscopic, etc)</span></li><li value="4"><span>People building stuff like “AI for Epistemics” or "AI for coordination"</span></li><li value="5"><span>External groups developing techniques to be exported to the labs (Redwood, Truthful AI, Cooperative AI Foundation, FAR.ai, Geodesic, most MATS projects)</span></li><li value="6"><span>External researchers who want to do theoretical alignment research (Sequent/Timaeus/Simplex, ARC, Iliad/PIBBSS-y stuff, formerly-MIRI people, Guaranteed Safe AI)</span></li><li value="7"><span>External researchers working on ambitious mech interp.</span></li><li value="8"><span>People trying to do pursue non-ASI paths to good futures (brain uploads, adult intelligence enhancement)</span></li><li value="9"><span>Academic labs (Stephen Casper, Vincent Conitzer, etc)</span></li><li value="10"><span>Engineers who want to build tooling for the labs and others (Trancluce, Meridian Labs, training better classifiers for control)</span></li><li value="11"><span>External groups who want to make the future better, not via AI safety (Forethought, Acorn, ACS)</span></li><li value="12"><span>External groups trying to solve technical bottlenecks to slowdown (hardware verification, tracking covert datacentres)</span></li><li value="13"><span>d/acc things (cyber-defense, bio-defense, societal hardening, formally-verified code)</span></li><li value="14"><span>Maybe some government organisations (EUAIO, CAISI, UK AISI)</span></li></ul><h2><span>Who aren’t outsiders?</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><b><span>The public — </span></b><span>Things would probably go better if the public had similar model access as the insiders. This would help with concentrations of power, wake up, and other risks described in Daniel Kokotajlo's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FGqfdJmB8MSH5LKGc/training-agi-in-secret-would-be-unsafe-and-unethical-1"><span>Training AGI in Secret would be Unsafe and Unethical</span></a><span>. But this article isn’t about that — it's just about model access for outsiders (which I think has different strategic properties).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Trailing labs </span></b><span>— It might also be good if employees at trailing also had access to the best internal models at the leading. Maybe, I haven't thought much about it. But I'm ignoring them because: (i) there are a bunch of thorny issues here, which don't arise with third-parties, and (ii) the upside is lower because (presumably) the trailing lab have their own models.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Government — </span></b><span>It might be good if the government had access to the best internal models. (Although, I'm a little worried if the government had much better access than the public.) In any case, this isn't my focus. Probably the government has a bunch of leverage over the labs which outsiders don't, so our interventions here would look pretty different.</span></li></ol><h1><span>What kinds of model access gap should we worry about?</span></h1><p><span>Here are five mechanisms that could contribute to a model access gap. I've ordered them in decreasing order of how severe I expect them to be.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Non-release</span></b><span>. The best internal models are either never deployed externally, or deployed only to partners I’m not focusing on (military, hardware suppliers, etc).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Deployment lag</span></b><span>. Models are eventually released, but available internally first, where this lag is enough to substantially hinder outsiders.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Safeguards</span></b><span>. Models are available, but wrapped in refusals and other policies that don't bind insiders.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Cost and rate limits</span></b><span>. Models are prohibitively priced or rate-limited.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Elicitation techniques (e.g. finetuning)</span></b><span>. You can't train the model on your specific task domain.</span></li></ol><p><span>I'll discuss these in more detail below.</span></p><h2><span>Non-release</span></h2><p><span>As of late June, the most powerful internal models are not available to the public at any price. And (to my knowledge) they aren't available to almsot any of the outsiders I listed above. This is probably the main development in the strategic landscape during Q2 2026.</span></p><p><span>I won't go through the timeline of the Mythos saga here (not least because it's still on-going), but the main takeaway is that the government will make it increasingly difficult for labs to release models, due to a combination of (i) competitiveness concerns, (ii) security concerns, (iii) preferential treatment of the labs.</span></p><p><span>My best guess is that Mythos-level models will be released to the public in the coming weeks. But I think it's plausible that this is the final generation of models which are widely deployed. And I think it's more likely than not that superhuman AI researchers are never widely deployed until the labs acheive ASI.</span></p><h2><span>Deployment lag</span></h2><p><span>Even if models are eventually released, they might be available internally several months earlier. Before the Mythos saga, these lags hadn't produced much of a model access gap: the gaps between the models is quite small, so it's not much hindrance to use the best available public model for a few weeks.</span></p><p><span>But deployment lags may be more worrying in the future:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>My guess is that deployment lags will grow. </span></b><span>This is mostly because the government will play a bigger role in the deployment decisions, and they a notoriously slow-moving entity. Also, the models will require more vetting and safeguards. There are some counterveiling effects (e.g. labs are more desperate for revenue/investment, they can accelerate their own pre-deployment evals, etc) but I expect these will be outweighed.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>A fixed deployment lag is a bigger deal when AI progress is superexponential.</span></b><span> Let Uplift(T)​ be the uplift from the best internal model at time T, and Uplift(T−δ) be uplift from the best model δ weeks earlier. Then if AI progresses superexponentially, then the ratio uplift(T)/uplift(T-delta) is increasing in T, i.e. the same wall-clock lag  would imply a larger uplift multiplier.</span></li></ol><p><span>Below, I've added some numbers from </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>AI Futures model</span></a><span>. Note that, I expect this table will overestimate the uplift gap, because outsiders will focus on domains with worse uplift than software R&amp;D.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><br /></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Software R&amp;D uplift</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>3 month lag</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>6 month</span></p></th></tr><tr style="height: 50px;"><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>June 2028</span></p></th><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2.8x (Automated Coder)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2x?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1.5x?</span></p></td></tr><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Feb 2029</span></p></th><td class="table-cell"><p><span>84x (TED-AI)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>9x</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3.3x</span></p></td></tr><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>May 2029</span></p></th><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1700x (ASI)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>84x</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>9x</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span>Safeguards</span></h2><p><span>As models get dangerous capabilities, labs will add more safeguards on model deployed publicly, e.g. refusal training, constitutional classifiers, etc. Especially if they are bound by RSPs, regulations, or reputational concerns. But the internal models will probably lack these safeguards. These safeguards might diminish the usefulness of the models.</span></p><p><span>It would be good if outsiders could make use of the best models without prohibitive safeguards. But historically, labs haven't been obliging about providing outsiders with access to non-public model access with diminished safeguards, e.g. helpful-only, deprecated models.</span></p><p><span>My best guess is that safeguards won't be a significant contributor to model access gap:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>I think this isn't biting right now. I haven't seen external safety researchers complain that refusals are blocking their work.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The insiders might also be hindered by the safeguards, e.g. because the labs are worried about insider threats or misaligned AIs.</span></li><li value="3"><span>I’m optimistic that KYC will solve this if it becomes an issue, c.f. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.09341"><span>Access Controls Will Solve the Dual-Use Dilemma</span></a></li></ol><p><span>That said, it's not implausible that safeguards become biting. And potentially, in 6-12 months, safeguards become one of the biggest bottlenecks on outsiders getting work done.</span></p><h2><span>Costs and rate limits</span></h2><p><span>Maybe a model is technically available to the public, but outsiders can't run it at the workload insiders do, because they are constrained by costs or rate limits. This would contribute to a model access gap.</span></p><p><span>We should expect the market-rate of frontier models to rise: AIs will use up more GPUs, because of training-time scaling and inference-time scaling; </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> the opportunity cost for GPUs will increase.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgboqff88goi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngboqff88goi">[4]</a></sup></span><span> See</span><a href="https://newsletter.danielpaleka.com/p/you-are-going-to-get-priced-out-of"><span> </span><i><span>You are going to get priced out of the best AI coding tools </span></i><span>(Daniel Paleka, 5th Nov 2025)</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>However, I think if labs provided the best internal models to outsiders with no markup, then I'm optimistic about the outsiders covering the rising costs. The outsiders will probably be able to spend billions of dollars during the ASI transition on making things go well. This is probably doable simply with philanthropic funding (supposing it's invested well). In particualr, if philanthropists expect to be spending most of their funding on compute, then they could invest in compute (and assets correlated with compute) as a hedge. If funders can’t cover the rising API costs, then we would need to lobby the labs or governments to subsidise compute, but this lobbying seems much more persuasive if we have the API access so we can demonstrate that the marginal utility of AI labour looks good.</span></p><h2><span>Elicitation techniques (e.g. finetuning)</span></h2><p><span>Lab employees probably have access to elicitation techniques like finetuning, RL training, scaffolding, etc. They'll use this to improve the models on the internal tasks, e.g. coding, architecture design, cybersecurity, etc. But if outsiders don't have access to the same elicitation techniques, then they may end up using a model that is less elicited for their use case.</span></p><p><span>I don't expect this to bite hard in practice. The internal models are presumably well-elicited for </span><i><span>internal</span></i><span> tasks, and I expect enough generalisation between the distribution of internal tasks and external tasks. So an external researcher with access to the internal model probably doesn’t get a big uplift boost from elicitation access.</span></p><p><span>It's possible outsiders want to do different kinds of work than the insiders (e.g. policy stuff, conceptual research, macrostrategy, hard-to-verify reasoning). Hence, the outsiders will enjoy less uplift than the insiders. But I don't expect that this capability limitation could be resolved simply by giving outsiders access to the same elicitation techniques as insiders. If this does occur, then I recommend outsiders simply construct benchmarks/evals/RL-tasks which they want labs to hill-climb.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RuGZ5tMdqpnraJahJ/model-access-for-third-parties-it-s-a-big-deal#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A CERN for AI is a distraction; push for an IAEA instead](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fPLCiCKjNiWhYD2mb/a-cern-for-ai-is-a-distraction-push-for-an-iaea-instead)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TL;DR: There are many conceivable versions of a “CERN for AI.” But the version that seems politically realistic (a new catch-up lab) probably would not do much for safety, while the versions that would materially improve safety (e.g., pause + merge of all companies) are probably unrealistic. So I see the CERN idea as a distraction, and not a particularly neglected one. I argue a better path is an international treaty with red lines now, with an IAEA-style verification body next: a sequencing that matches how the EU AI Act, the NPT/IAEA, and the Montreal Protocol actually developed. This is premised on the view that the main bottleneck in AI safety is enforcement and political will, not more R&amp;D.</span></b></p><p><span>Two premises underlie the rationale below:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>First, the bottleneck in AI safety is political will and the enforcement of best practices, not more R&amp;D. </span></b><span>With enough will, we move from </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E8n93nnEaFeXTbHn5/plans-a-b-c-and-d-for-misalignment-risk"><span>Greenblatt's Plan D toward Plan A</span></a><span>, achieving roughly an 80% risk reduction. Of course, the science of AI safety is far from mature, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YFxpsrph83H25aCLW/the-80-20-playbook-for-mitigating-ai-scheming-in-2025#The_Real_Bottleneck__Enforcement"><span>but we are also far from applying the best risk mitigation practices</span></a><span> (see also the various other ratings, from SaferAI to FLI’s one) - of course, alignment is not solved, but also, xAI is still releasing Mecha Hitler…</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>We already have sufficient verification mechanisms to get started. </span></b><span>What's missing is the decision to use them (</span><a href="https://x.com/CRSegerie/status/2003122396799398031"><span>link</span></a><span>).</span></li></ul><p><span>If you reject either premise, you might reach different conclusions.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><h2><span>The CERN for AI is a distraction</span></h2></li></ol><p><span>A recurring proposal in AI governance is to build a “CERN for AI”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa34sxwowla6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna34sxwowla6">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. The CERN pitch is seductive. "</span><i><span>Let's build together!</span></i><span>" That's sexier than "</span><i><span>we need to ban.</span></i><span>" You can leverage historical analogies (CERN for physics, NASA) and talk about national interest and science. It sounds like the smarter, more sophisticated play.[1]</span></p><p><span>But I think that there are many problems with it.</span></p><p><b><span>What do you even mean by CERN?</span></b></p><p><span>Are you asking for:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>a) pausing AI development at OpenAI, etc, and on top of this pause, creating a new institution that conducts ALL the frontier development?</span></b><span> Let's be clear: this will never happen unless you </span><i><span>explicitly</span></i><span> ask for a pause. And by default, the US and US CEOs will push extremely hard against handing off their power in this way</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref61zyrimp7vl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn61zyrimp7vl">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. (I’m not even sure this would be desirable in terms of power concentration, and I’m not sure such a structure would be able to do a better job than Anthropic at pushing frontier AI safety R&amp;D). Push for (a) without saying 'pause' and you'll get (b) by default:</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>b) a new lab that tries to catch up to frontier labs.</span></b><span> But this new lab, trying to catch up, is not reducing risks. Also, every state's attempt at frontier LLMs has been 2-3 generations behind the labs. The comparative advantage of states isn't racing frontier labs. It's regulating them. A CERN asks states to do the one thing they're worst at. A CERN-for-AI in Europe today would most likely look like a new Mistral, and competitors who try to catch up tend to cut corners on safety and lobby against regulation (cf. Mistral).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>c) or maybe you want to create a literal CERN</span></b><span>, i.e., a pure research center, which would not necessarily create frontier models? But there is already plenty of research that is being ignored by companies. I believe the bottleneck is currently enforcement and binding regulation. A research center without enforcement teeth doesn't shift industry incentives.</span></li></ul><p><span>To be honest, if you really do believe we might face catastrophic risks in a few months or years, I think the priority is to stop the hemorrhage</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefspqhysyrg8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnspqhysyrg8">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. If what you ultimately want is to mitigate AI risks, say it, and don't play 4D chess.</span></p><p><span>We should ask for international regulations now. This leads us to:</span></p><ol start="2"><li value="2"><h2><span>Red lines now, IAEA for AI next</span></h2></li></ol><p><i><span>For context, the IAEA (International Atomic Energy Agency) issues international nuclear </span></i><i><b><span>safety standards and red lines</span></b></i><i><span>, supports peer reviews and inspections, and coordinates assistance during nuclear emergencies. These standards are then adopted and enforced through national legislation worldwide. An IAEA for AI would play a similar role for artificial intelligence.</span></i></p><p><b><span>Red lines have something CERN doesn't: existing momentum.</span></b></p><p><span>Red lines are the </span><a href="https://thefuturesociety.org/cso-ai-governance-priorities/"><span>most widely supported</span></a><span> measure by research institutes, think tanks, and independent organizations. By signing the Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments, companies agreed to "Set out thresholds at which severe risks posed by a model or system, unless adequately mitigated, would be deemed intolerable." Granted, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6CYszKLnCagYyEiLM/openai-s-red-line-for-ai-self-improvement-is-fundamentally"><span>OpenAI's "red line" for recursive AI self-improvement is currently inadequate</span></a><span>, but we're not building from zero, and this is why red lines need to be binding rather than voluntary.</span></p><p><span>China's Premier Li Qiang </span><a href="https://www.barrons.com/news/china-premier-says-red-line-needed-in-ai-development-3116339a"><span>stated that</span></a><span> "there should be a red line in AI development, a red line that must not be crossed." Pope Francis urged nations to adopt "</span><a href="https://www.reuters.com/technology/pope-calls-binding-global-treaty-artificial-intelligence-2023-12-14/"><span>a binding international treaty</span></a><span>.”, and Paolo Benanti, the Pope's AI adviser, called explicitly for "</span><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/paolo-benanti-catholic-church-vatican-superintelligence-artificial-intelligence-pope"><span>binding international treaties and red lines.</span></a><span>"</span></p><p><span>Even the Executive order signed by Trump in early June frames this as a threshold definition: NSA will define a cyber-capability threshold, classified and shared with developers.</span></p><p><b><span>Red lines need an institution: the IAEA model</span></b></p><p><span>The final big hesitation while drafting the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vKA2BgpESFZSHaQnT/global-call-for-ai-red-lines-signed-by-nobel-laureates"><span>Global Call for AI Red Lines</span></a><span> was whether to ask explicitly for the IAEA for AI. The main reason we didn't ask for the IAEA in the global call was mostly to generate more support ("IAEA for AI" sounds technocratic and wonky to non-specialists, while red lines are intuitive). But red lines only matter if they can be independently verified: without an independent authority, there is no trust; without trust, no credible verification; and without credible verification, red lines are unlikely to be respected. This is why the strongest arms-control regimes are paired with international verification mechanisms: the Chemical Weapons Convention with the OPCW, and the NPT with the IAEA.</span></p><p><span>At the India AI Impact Summit, the CEOs of the three leading frontier AI labs each called for international AI oversight.</span><a href="https://x.com/CRSegerie/status/2024570328295625153"><span> </span></a><a href="https://x.com/CRSegerie/status/2024570328295625153"><span>Altman joined Hassabis</span></a><span> in calling for an institution modeled on the IAEA. Amodei called for red lines with enforcement mechanisms.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffk5835gj5tt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfk5835gj5tt">[4]</a></sup></span><span> The fact that CEOs have recently publicly called for IAEA-style oversight is one of the strongest arguments for the current US administration.</span></p><p><span>This sequencing –</span><i><span> an international agreement with red lines first, institution second</span></i><span> – mirrors how international governance actually works. The EU AI Act passed without every technical threshold defined; the AI Office was established afterward; specific thresholds are currently being defined, by technical </span><a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/funding/eu-ai-office-launches-eu9-million-tender-technical-support-gpai-safety"><span>consortiums working with the AI Office</span></a><span>. Same pattern from the Vienna Convention to the Montreal Protocol, with detailed control measures strengthened gradually through expert-led review. Political agreement creates the conditions for technical work to happen inside the governance process, not before it.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Arthur Grimonpont and Epiphanie Gedeon for helpful suggestions and feedback on this memo.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fna34sxwowla6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa34sxwowla6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Even Demis Hassabis, back in the day,</span><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/singularity/comments/1iseeqz/google_deepmind_ceo_says_for_agi_to_go_well/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/singularity/comments/1iseeqz/google_deepmind_ceo_says_for_agi_to_go_well/"><span>said</span></a><span>: "At some point in the future, we'll need a CERN for AGI for international coordination on safety research."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn61zyrimp7vl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref61zyrimp7vl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If a credible pause-plus-co-development package were actually on the table (something like the AI-2027 slowdown scenario, with DeepMind, Anthropic and OpenAI brought into one project, even though they cannot hold hands at the Delhi Summit), I'd be happy to back option (a). I don't expect that scenario to materialize. CEOs of frontier companies have way too much ego for this. Even Mistral, which is already generations behind, would resist being folded into a frontier consortium tomorrow. Merging the different frontier labs is wayyyy harder than creating a new institution. The EU AI Office creation went pretty smoothly when you think about it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnspqhysyrg8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefspqhysyrg8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><b><span>Many people pushing for a CERN have European sovereignty in mind.</span></b><span> To be fair, I think that Europe should wake up to the importance of AI. But there are so many ways to do it in a more effective way:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>If what you want is </span><b><span>sovereignty</span></b><span>, the easiest version is to package open source models that are currently just 4 months behind the frontier - not train frontier models from scratch that are 3 generations behind!</span></li><li value="2"><span>If you want </span><b><span>safety</span></b><span>: enforce the AI act, and serve as a diplomatic power to get safety on the world scene.</span></li><li value="3"><span>If you want to fund </span><b><span>moonshot for alignment</span></b><span>, I’m very skeptical this is the most direct route</span></li><li value="4"><span>If you want to strengthen your </span><b><span>industry</span></b><span>, prepare for physical AI and robotics</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfk5835gj5tt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffk5835gj5tt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The CEOs of the three leading AI companies have each publicly called for international oversight. Dario Amodei said he could imagine </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/02/12/opinion/artificial-intelligence-anthropic-amodei.html"><span>a worldwide treaty with enforcement mechanisms</span></a><span>. Sam Altman called for "</span><a href="https://x.com/AFP/status/2024471561764291071"><span>urgent global regulation on AI</span></a><span>”, and for </span><a href="https://x.com/AFP/status/2024471561764291071"><span>an equivalent of the International Atomic Energy Agency for international coordination on AI</span></a><span>. Demis Hassabis also called for "</span><a href="https://youtu.be/4poqjZlM8Lo?si=6ZZrlspr27zaost_"><span>some kind of equivalent of the IAEA</span></a><span>." For reference, the IAEA issues international nuclear safety standards and red lines, supports peer reviews and inspections, and coordinates assistance during nuclear emergencies. These standards are then adopted and enforced through national legislation worldwide.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fPLCiCKjNiWhYD2mb/a-cern-for-ai-is-a-distraction-push-for-an-iaea-instead#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [You Should Come to The AI Protest](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4kqbNCMCkaSJTigii/you-should-come-to-the-ai-protest)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>cart;horse:</span></b><span> If you are in the Bay Area on July 11th, even if you're at a company being protested, you should come to </span><a href="https://theprotest.ai/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The AI Protest</span></a><span>. It's fully legal and nonviolent (we'll have a full overtime SFPD escort the entire time), and it's not the worst way you can use your Saturday afternoon that weekend. Plus all of your coolest friends will be there.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>There's a lot of discussion about the effectiveness of protests and marches; I don't want to re-litigate that here when you can just ask your favorite model.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2vv9fo0jmn9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2vv9fo0jmn9">[1]</a></sup></span><span> There's also lots of existing discussion on if/how/when we should pause, I'll point you to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mEhS4wYTy9JXEpe9p/ai-pause-the-case-for-asap" rel="noreferrer"><span>Katja's recent post</span></a><span> and the discussion there as a start for that. This is a review of some concerns I have had or still somewhat have about this type of action, and how I've reasoned about and gotten through them. I imagine you might also have these concerns.</span></p><p><span>The ask (stopping the race) is one that a large fraction of the public is likely supportive of, with </span><a href="https://yougov.com/en-us/articles/54762-most-americans-say-artificial-intelligence-ai-development-moving-too-fast-twice-as-many-ai-pessimists-as-ai-optimists-may-9-11-2026-economist-yougov-poll" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the majority concerned about the current pace of development</span></a><span>. Of course there is a ton of behind-the-scenes inside-baseball work to be done and the details do matter, but we do need to build the pressure and steer the conversation from the outside, so that those on the inside get the resources they need towards making it happen. The larger the demand that frontier AI companies, governments, and other actors put forth concrete proposals and start coordinating, the faster that it will happen.</span></p><h2><span>Coalitions and Communication</span></h2><p><span>There will be people who care about data center water usage. There will be people who explicitly never use AI. There will be people who are concerned about CSAM and child suicide.</span></p><p><span>But this protest is about a push for a conditional pause, specifically because of transformative AI leading to the value of human labor going to zero, gradual disempowerment and concentration of power, and catastrophic and existential risks.</span></p><p><span>Coalition building is a necessary part of dramatic and positive change, and this march is a first step toward that. This is a fantastic opportunity to understand and shape the conversations happening outside the EA/Rat/AI bubble; many people there won't be reading the EA Forum or LessWrong. The more they see the passion we bring to naming these risks, and that we're all fighting for the same values, the more likely we are to succeed.</span></p><h2><span>A Few Rebuttals</span></h2><p><b><span>"This will affect my future career prospects at [frontier company]."</span></b><span> Realistically, your participation is unlikely to be used against you: a background check coming back "attended anti-AI protest" and costing you a job isn't a serious threat model (a potential exception being you have strong evidence your counterfactual impact depends on a specific job, and strong reason to believe that employer would actually reject you over this). There are enough people trying to play the inside game, and I think we need those on the outside to be playing their part more strongly (making their opinion louder). Plus you don't have to worry about value drift :).</span></p><p><b><span>"This looks like I'm protesting my own [frontier company]."</span></b><span> The protest is specifically focused on </span><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Stopping the AI Race</span></a><span>, and your company has gestured at this in principle but has stopped short of committing. You're not there in opposition to your employer; you're there in support of a shared goal. The hard work of operationalizing a slowdown/pause/stop still has to happen, but the more pressure exists to make it happen, the faster and more seriously it will be taken.</span></p><p><b><span>"I'll be photographed or identified, or I could face HR or reputational consequences."</span></b><span> It's possible. There are protections for at-will employees under California law (</span><a href="https://california.public.law/codes/labor_code_section_1101" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Labor Code 1101/1102</span></a><span>), but you might be under some other term that makes this effectively moot. There's nonzero risk here, but you can attend without being the story. There will be hundreds of people there and the news cycle will likely move on immediately. There's also power in numbers! Get coworkers and come conditionally: if ten of you come, it's now about "employees attending", not about you specifically.</span></p><p><b><span>"I'm at [frontier company], I'll be called a hypocrite." </span></b><span>This is exactly the kind of credible signal that </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> people with knowledge "on the inside" can send. The nurses, artists, and climate advocates at this march don't have the credibility to say "I understand the capabilities; I'm building them; and I'm still asking you to slow down." You do. That's a HUGE signal to the broader public and to policymakers about what kinds of humans cared enough to show up, and their credibility. This is an easy way you can leverage your position to effect positive change on the outside, without having to actually leak anything.</span></p><p><b><span>"The crowd will be radical and I'll be associated with it."</span></b><span> There probably will be people saying "Fuck OpenAI" and "Quit Your Job." There will be rough takes, and speeches or chants that come from a perspective you don't specifically endorse. This is a part of coalition building, and while group dynamics may come into play, most people will have individual perspectives that you can both learn from and help inform! Speaking to these people in person, as people, will make the nuance you and I care about come through more strongly. If you stay home to avoid the noise, the noise is all that's left.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Attending, in my opinion, is brave: you have values, you want to shape the world toward them, and you'll compromise with others and take on some personal risk for a shared cause. In the situation where everyone reasons the other way (i.e., if all of us stay home to protect our own standing), no one is left to say in public what they believe in private: a far worse outcome than being occasionally spotted at this protest.</span></p><p><span>This is a moment where your values, the frontier companies' stated values, and a broad public coalition are starting to point in roughly the same direction. This doesn't happen often, and your marginal attendance can help steer that rough direction. Please don't sit it out!</span></p><p><span>The march is happening on Saturday, July 11 from 12–3 PM, starting at the OpenAI headquarters through downtown to Anthropic and DeepMind, with speeches at all three. More details at:</span></p><p><a href="https://theprotest.ai/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://theprotest.ai/</span></a></p><p><a href="https://stoptherace.ai/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://stoptherace.ai/</span></a></p><p><a href="https://luma.com/s0k8wvee?utm_source=ronak_eaflwpost" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Luma</span></a><span> - </span><a href="https://partiful.com/e/EChgbBMsoeN3qVnATHKJ?utm_source=ronak_eaflwpost" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Partiful</span></a></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2vv9fo0jmn9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2vv9fo0jmn9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some first pointers synthesized from a first Claude pass (links verified):</span></p><p><span>On the effectiveness of protest and its limits, see </span><a href="https://cup.columbia.edu/book/why-civil-resistance-works/9780231156837/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Chenoweth &amp; Stephan, </span><i><span>Why Civil Resistance Works</span></i></a><span>, whose 1900–2006 dataset finds nonviolent campaigns roughly twice as likely to succeed as violent ones (and the source of the widely cited "3.5% rule"); </span><a href="https://press.princeton.edu/books/hardcover/9780691181776/the-loud-minority" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Gillion, </span><i><span>The Loud Minority</span></i></a><span>, which treats protest as a signal that measurably moves legislative votes, voter turnout, and elections; </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/9781009074322" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Gause, </span><i><span>The Advantage of Disadvantage</span></i></a><span>, arguing that costly protest by low-resource and marginalized groups draws </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> responsiveness from legislators, not less; and </span><a href="https://www.upress.umn.edu/9780816629152/how-social-movements-matter/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Giugni (ed.), </span><i><span>How Social Movements Matter</span></i></a><span>, a useful check on all of the above for how hard it is to show movements actually </span><i><span>caused</span></i><span> the outcomes credited to them. For the skeptical counterpoint, see </span><a href="https://www.twitterandteargas.org/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Tufekci, </span><i><span>Twitter and Tear Gas</span></i></a><span> (</span><a href="https://www.twitterandteargas.org/downloads/twitter-and-tear-gas-by-zeynep-tufekci.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>freely available online</span></a><span>), which argues that digital tools let movements assemble enormous marches quickly without building the organizational capacity that sustained older movements — so sheer size can mask underlying fragility.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4kqbNCMCkaSJTigii/you-should-come-to-the-ai-protest#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Green](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NyKqHj5C9nRX2bvAC/green)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>1</span></h1><p><span>Alice: My favorite color is green.</span></p><p><span>Bob: Oh, cool. Mine's red.</span></p><p><i><span>Months later...</span></i></p><p><span>Bob: Hey, I got you this green painting. I remember you saying your favorite color is green and I thought you'd like it.</span></p><p><span>Alice: Oh. Um, that's nice of you, but I actually don't really like green. My favorite color's not green — it's blue.</span></p><p><span>Bob: Oh. I coulda sworn you had said green.</span></p><p><span>Alice: Oh, I think I did say green. But, um, Bob... you really shouldn't take things people say so literally all of the time.</span></p><p><span>Bob: </span><i><span>Blinks. </span></i><span>I'm confused.</span></p><p><span>Alice: Ok. Well, I mean, yeah, I think I did say green. But, like, I was just kinda gesturing at the area where my favorite color is. And I obviously didn't mean, like, literally green.</span></p><p><span>Bob: Obviously?</span></p><p><span>Alice: Yeah. Green is a secondary color. Who chooses a secondary color as their favorite color. My favorite color is obviously going to be a primary color.</span></p><p><span>Bob: Ok. That was quite far from being obvious to me, but ok. But — and I have a feeling you're going to accuse me of being too analytical for saying this — but even if I infered that your favorite color must actually be a primary color, wouldn't it still be ambiguous whether it's blue or yellow?</span></p><p><span>Alice: Ugh. Sorry, but yeah, you are being too analytical. Sure — if you want to be technical about it, yes, green is equidistant from blue and from yellow if you look at hue angles on a color wheel. But why would you look at hue angles on a color wheel! We're humans!</span></p><p><span>Bob: I wish I understood what you mean.</span></p><p><span>Alice: Ok. At least in my experience, like, yellow is kinda gross. I mean, who's favorite color is yellow? Blue is clearly a much more aesthetically pleasing and likable color.</span></p><h1><span>2</span></h1><p><span>Carol: My favorite color is green.</span></p><p><span>Dave: I'm sorry — green? Just, green?</span></p><p><span>Carol: Uh, yeah. Why... you seem confused?</span></p><p><span>Dave: I mean, that just isn't very precise. It could mean so many different things. People measure color in various ways — wavelength, hue, physiological encodings, whatever. But whichever model you pick, just saying "green" only tells me so much.</span></p><p><span>Carol: Ok, now I'm confused.</span></p><p><span>Dave: Well, there's a blog post called </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yDfxTj9TKYsYiWH5o/the-virtue-of-narrowness" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Virtue of Narrowness</span></a><span> that you really should read, but I'll elaborate. Imagine a space of all possible colors. When you said "green", you drew a circle around a set of — let's call them points — a set of points in color space. This set contains many, many, many points, and so you failed to effectively zero in on the concept you were intending to convey to me, which is poor communication.</span></p><p><span>Carol: I —</span></p><p><span>Dave: Eliezer has a blot post called </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SXK87NgEPszhWkvQm/mundane-magic" rel="noreferrer"><span>Mundane Magic</span></a><span> where he dives into an adjacent idea that you may find to be helpful. He refers to speaking as "vibratory telepathy". You see, when we speak, we vibrate air molecules in such a way where one person — the "sender", we can call them — shares a </span><i><span>thought</span></i><span> with the other person, the "receiver". So by vibrating air molecules, you can actually take a mind-state in one person and cause a second person to experience that same mind-state. It's pretty cool.</span></p><p><span>Carol: I think I see —</span></p><p><span>Dave: And your failure was that, in the way you vibrated those air molecules, it didn't actually allow me to experience the same mind-state as you. You should have vibrated them in a different way. A way that —</span></p><p><span>Carol: Dave! Slow down a second. I sorta get what you mean, but this is getting very abstract.</span></p><p><span>Dave: Ok, I think I can dumb it down for you. Sorry.</span></p><p><span>Carol: I —</span></p><p><span>Dave: Ok, imagine that you ask me where I live. With me so far?</span></p><p><span>Carol: Sure.</span></p><p><span>Dave: Good. And imagine that I just say... "the United States".</span></p><p><span>Carol: Ok.</span></p><p><span>Dave: Cool, you're doing good. That wouldn't be very helpful... would it?</span></p><p><span>Carol: It depends on </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> I am asking the question, but sure — I agree that it wouldn't be very helpful.</span></p><p><span>Dave: Good.</span></p><p><span>Carol: </span><i><span>Blinks.</span></i></p><p><span>Dave: Well? Do you not see the parallel? I don't know how to — you basically just told me that your favorite color is "the United States".</span></p><p><span>Carol: Actually, I think what I told you is that my favorite color is Portland.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NyKqHj5C9nRX2bvAC/green#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Structural Proxies](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mKiBhFJs3MoksaBMs/structural-proxies)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Lately I've been thinking a lot about what work would help with actually winning and getting to good worlds. In the spirit of that I decided to venture outside my normal wheelhouse and spend some time reflecting on what technical research could make me more confident about powerful AIs being safe.</span></p><p><span>AGI safety research is tricky partly because we don’t actually have access to the thing we want to study, i.e. superhuman AI. Much of the work we do now is basically trying to lay (potentially irrelevant) foundations for the period when we actually know what we’re up against, and at that point, a lot of the work might be done by AIs.</span></p><p><span>You can group current approaches by how they try to sidestep this access problem:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhuyhgdgc85"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhuyhgdgc85">[1]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Prosaic</span></b><span> techniques like RLHF and interpretability try to make progress on current model safety in a way that will hopefully generalise, except maybe they just won’t scale</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Model organisms</span></b><span> artificially construct exemplars of bad behaviour (alignment faking, trojans etc) but it’s hard to tell how representative the constructed case is</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Control</span></b><span> techniques aim to get usable work out of potentially misaligned AIs and bootstrapping, except it’s again unclear how far that will scale</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Agent foundations</span></b><span> tries to reason theoretically about what powerful AIs will be like, except it’s really hard to be sure if you’ve made any useful progress</span></li><li value="5"><i><span>Honourable mentions</span></i><span>: Evals work, Scalable Oversight (RIP), Governance, Pause advocacy etc…</span></li></ul><p><span>Here I want to gesture at a different angle of attack, which I’m going to call </span><b><span>structural proxies</span></b><span>. The basic idea is that you look for current naturally-appearing problems with AI that share a structure with future problems — in other words, structural proxies. In particular, you want to have some reason to believe that the current problem is being generated by the same types of dynamics that will produce the future problem, even if the manifestations look quite different. You then use the proxies to try to understand the future problems. </span></p><p><span>The closest parallel is model organisms — it's plausible to me that this is just a special subset. But a lot of model organism work appears to start from the specific phenomenon (reward hacking, alignment faking, collusion...) and then deliberately construct an environment that demonstrates it. With structural proxies, you start from the process that produces the phenomenon (misgeneralisation, latent knowledge, optimisation for proxies...) and then look for other examples of that process in the wild.</span></p><p><span>Briefly, I think this is a useful angle because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Very generally it’s good to have more angles of attack</span></li><li value="2"><span>It’s especially useful as we enter the era of automated engineering</span></li><li value="3"><span>Although you have more constraints on what you can proxy, the proxies have the potential to be pretty faithful, and therefore to give you a lot of evidence</span></li><li value="4"><span>In particular, this could help us spot problems or places where things don’t scale</span></li><li value="5"><span>Conversely, getting a rich understanding of a structural proxy for one of the endgame technical challenges actually would make me feel better about alignment</span></li></ul><p><span>I’ll run through two examples now — adversarial attacks as a proxy for value generalisation, and faithfulness as a proxy for ELK — then give some broader thoughts.</span></p><h2><span>Adversarial attacks as a proxy for value generalisation</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3n8fvewaymm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3n8fvewaymm">[2]</a></sup></span></h2><p><span>Conceptually, what an adversarial attack does is exploit the gap between what we want the model to learn and what it’s actually learned — in other words, exploit its misgeneralisation. In the case of image models, producing adversarial inputs that work across a variety of models requires you to exploit the general ways that image models learn differently to humans.</span></p><p><span>Interestingly, these differences appear to be somewhat natural and intuitive byproducts of the fact that image models train on static images whereas humans see things continually — to the point that some adversarial images </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.08195"><span>also “fool” humans who only look at them for a fraction of a second</span></a><span>. I think this is spiritually similar to the way that feature visualisation on image models by default gives you a horrid static mess, but starts to look recognisable once you regularise for an input that’s </span><a href="https://distill.pub/2017/feature-visualization/"><span>robust to small translations and other perturbations</span></a><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782821434/lexical_client_uploads/ubjyqqedx60jhrfe0f5q.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Distill pub has </span></i><a href="https://distill.pub/2017/feature-visualization/"><i><span>live demos where you can vary the parameters</span></i></a><i><span> — highly recommended if you want to get a feel for how jittering affects human interpretability.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Language models are a bit of a different ballgame, because the input space isn’t quite so densely high-dimensional — for an image model, a slight perturbation means an almost imperceptible pixel shift, whereas for a language model it probably means an embedding that doesn’t correspond to any tokens. Nonetheless, I think the shape of the argument is similar: Jailbreaking shouldn’t be viewed as a strange epicycle on otherwise-sensible models, but rather as a sign that very fundamentally language models are generalising differently to the way we’d want them to — in other words, </span><a href="https://www.gleech.org/jailbreak"><span>they are misaligned</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>And indeed, it seems like by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.15043"><span>greedily searching for token substitutions</span></a><span> one can do something similar to tweaking pixels on images — producing model-agnostic suffixes for text prompts that force certain behaviours. The good news is that, much like the image model case, this is much harder if you </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15735"><span>regularise the inputs into being somewhat comprehensible</span></a><span>, to the point that sometimes the shape of the attack becomes human-understandable.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782821435/lexical_client_uploads/bwlrkevbdldcxge1sxrd.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Human classification can also be flipped by surprisingly small tweaks — spot the difference.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Taking a step back: it seems like the standard orientation to adversarial attacks is that they’re mostly a misuse problem, where people are using incomprehensible blobs of noise to hijack otherwise benign models — models that have been aligned against a really diverse range of examples and validated on a really diverse range of examples. In other words, one might think that adversarial attacks are shallow, and the actual deep thing is constitutional learning and eval performance.</span></p><p><span>But I think there’s a reasonable case to be made that it’s the other way around — that adversarial fragility is the deep signal, and eval performance is a shallow layer on top — and that blocking jailbreaks on language models and exploits on image models is hard for the same structural reason that alignment is hard.</span></p><p><span>The million dollar question here</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefl1tyiyarf8n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnl1tyiyarf8n">[3]</a></sup></span><span> is what the limit of this process looks like. For example, does there exist a training process which gives you image models that classify things exactly the way a human would, including in worst-case adversarial inputs? If so, I think that would make me a fair bit more optimistic about alignment. I expect that much of what you learned in building such a system would be pretty generalisable.</span></p><p><span>That’s the structural proxy angle: taking a known, natural issue (adversarial robustness) and noticing the ways it’s structurally similar to a big future problem (value generalisation), and using the proxy to get purchase on the future problem. Notably, lots of traditional adversarial robustness work like layering smaller classifiers on top is not at all useful for pursuing the proxy angle; on the other hand, plenty of adversarial robustness work that’s not super inefficient for actually preventing misuse suddenly looks really appealing from this angle, like trying to find conditions that make optimised jailbreaks more naturally interpretable, or trying to train models in a way that makes optimised inputs less like static.</span></p><h2><span>Faithfulness as a proxy for ELK</span></h2><p><span>The problem of </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit?tab=t.0"><span>Eliciting Latent Knowledge</span></a><span> (ELK) was ARC’s big attempt to give a formal, tractable variant of the alignment problem: if you have an AI that knows a bunch of stuff, can you train a system that will report that knowledge? Solving this gets you most of the way to solving alignment because you can just ask your misaligned system questions like “are you misaligned” and “is this going to go wrong” and actually elicit all the latent important details.</span></p><p><span>And the basic challenge is that it’s really hard to be confident you’ll actually get an “direct translator” that expresses the latent knowledge in the AI, instead of a “human simulator” that figures out what you’d expect to hear and just tells you that. For example, no question/answer pairs from you can privilege “tell us the truth” as a strategy over “tell us what we believe the truth is”, especially once the model is superhuman. Worse, it’s possible that figuring out and extrapolating the most sophisticated world model humans might develop could be much easier than extrapolating over the one true model that ASI will learn.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrrevvnk99t9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrrevvnk99t9">[4]</a></sup></span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782821435/lexical_client_uploads/mrll1rkvto6x5lqy4xwr.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Cribbed from the (very readable) </span></i><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit?tab=t.0"><i><span>ELK report</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>This was originally posed as a hypothetical problem, and most of the work on it has been fairly abstract — ARC’s current approach is mostly about finding algorithms for predicting the behaviours of different networks in a principled way that’s more efficient than just sampling them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk1gyudq9op"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk1gyudq9op">[5]</a></sup></span><span> But I think one can learn quite a lot about it from looking at current systems — indeed, I think we’ve already learned a fair bit.</span></p><p><span>For example, we know:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.13382"><span>Neural nets can learn world models which you can probe</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.07958"><span>Base models repeat common misconceptions, and this can increase with scale</span></a><span> (this is actually where the term inverse scaling was coined!)</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13548"><span>LMs mirror the beliefs of their users</span></a></li><li value="4"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15674"><span>LMs can be trained to somewhat accurately report the meanings of internal computations</span></a></li></ul><p><span>People already care a lot about whether AIs are honest. This has the advantage of being easily testable whenever you have some ground truth to hand. But for ELK I think the better proxy is faithfulness — not whether the output is correct, but whether the process generating it was coupled to the right internal structure.</span></p><p><span>Here it’s useful to consider what work would be considered out of scope. One good example is consistency. Lots of people are into making models consistent across conversations — to reduce </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.27062"><span>sycophancy and jailbreaks</span></a><span>, make them better at </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fi3dtMrXJiWEtmJNa/a-preliminary-experiment-regarding-consistency-as-a-measure"><span>conceptual reasoning</span></a><span>, or even </span><a href="https://x.com/jiaxinwen22/status/2062773614920040818"><span>find out what they really think</span></a><span>. If you put on your ELK hat, though, it’s clear that this is only half the battle. Consistency makes AIs pick </span><i><span>one</span></i><span> world model, but does it make them pick the </span><i><span>right</span></i><span> one? (Spoiler alert: </span><a href="https://x.com/DavidDAfrica/status/2062205694288540012"><span>maybe not</span></a><span>.)</span></p><p><span>Now, solving ELK for GPT3 is pretty different to solving it for arbitrary superintelligences. My point is that you could still </span><i><span>try</span></i><span> to solve it, or to figure out what bits are hard. Do AIs even </span><i><span>have</span></i><span> a true model of the world, from which they model what humans expect to hear? How does this connect to the fact that current AIs are a bit more like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zuXo9imNKYspu9HGv/a-three-layer-model-of-llm-psychology"><span>layers of prediction</span></a><span> with some ability for the character to tap the raw capacities?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefavpkq42salu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnavpkq42salu">[6]</a></sup></span><span> Can we find the causal channels when AIs </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> simulating ‘inaccurate’ reporters? How does all this vary with model size and post-training?</span></p><p><span>So, recapping the angle: take a known, natural problem (unfaithful reporting), noticing the ways it’s structurally similar to a future problem (ELK), and use the proxy to get purchase.</span></p><h2><span>Thoughts on structural proxies as a research direction</span></h2><p><span>My big ambitious hope is that this kind of work could actually make us more or less confident about the difficulty of aligning very powerful AIs. I worry that a lot of safety work bounces off the hard parts of the problem, and I like the idea of actually grabbing at whatever hard parts you can find. It would be very useful to have compelling evidence that we’re not yet equipped for the hard challenges, by providing smaller but not much easier instantiations. But there's also a lot of upside — if we </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> solve jailbreaking in full generality, or we </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> figure out exactly how faithfulness works in current models, that would be big. I have a hunch that when you work on simplified versions of the problem, sometimes you risk simplifying away not just the things that make them difficult but also the things that make them solvable.</span></p><p><span>More mundanely, I just think it's good to have as many angles of attack as possible. Especially as we enter the era of automated research, it's good to have a plan for what we point the auto-engineers at. It's also good to have approaches with different strengths and weaknesses so that the overall portfolio is a bit more robust.</span></p><p><span>This work is notably predicated on the assumption that the mechanisms producing today's problems are somewhat continuous with the mechanisms that will produce tomorrow's. It is possible that actually things just won’t scale, or that crunch time will involve very different paradigms. It is also possible (likely, even!) that more powerful AIs will exhibit qualitatively new challenges that don’t have easy analogues, like advanced forms of situational awareness. This cluster of work, by construction, can only attack problems with some degree of continuity, so it’s not as much of a full package as something like AI control.</span></p><p><span>Still, that's only a limit on how much optimism it can give us: there's still plenty of pessimism to try to squeeze out. Put another way, I think we should plan for problems scaling better than solutions. In particular, if we are headed to a world of abundant automated engineering, it could be useful to find more conceptually ways of red teaming how our existing approaches might break at a slightly higher level of abstraction. And if our plans can survive all that, I'll feel pretty good.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to JK, CG, NN, VK, JB, and DA for many comments and much discussion.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhuyhgdgc85"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a separable — lots of current control work is based on making model organisms, model organism work relies on prosaic techniques, etc</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" ## Engineering

### [Building Reliable Agentic AI Systems](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>One of the most interesting projects my colleagues have done with LLMs
      has been building a system with Bayer to allow pharmaceutical researchers to query
      decades of information about studies buried in PDF reports. <b class="author">Sarang
      Sanjay Kulkarni</b> describes its evolution from keyword-based search
      to an intelligent research assistant capable of answering complex
      questions and drafting regulatory documents.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reliable-llm-bayer.html">more…</a></p>
